SPECIFICATION\nTENDER NO. NC/15/2026\nTHE CONSTRUCTION OF 12 BNG HOUSES IN ROSEDALE 400, DAWID KRUIPER\nLOCAL MUNICIPALITY FOR THE ZF MGCAWU DISTRICT MUNICIPALITY\n1. HEALTH AND SAFETY SPECIFICATION\n1.1 Scope\nThis Health & Safety Specification has been developed to address all aspects of occupational\nhealth and safety, as affected by the proposed construction work in accordance with the provisions\nin the Construction Regulations.\nThe specification provides the requirements that the Principal Contractor and other Contractors\nshall have to comply with to reduce the risks associated with the construction work to a level as\nlow as reasonably practicable.\n1.2 Introduction\nIn terms of Construction Regulation 5(1) (b) and (c) of the Occupational Health and Safety Act,\nNo. 85 of 1993, the Client, or his Health and Safety Agent, is required to compile a Site-Specific\nHealth & Safety Specification for any intended project and provide such specification to the\nDesigner as well as to any prospective tenderers.\nThe Client's further duties are stipulated in Clause 3, and in the Construction Regulations,\npublished in Government Gazette No 37305 of 2014. This specification has an objective to ensure\nthat Principal Contractors and other Contractors entering in to a Contract with the Client, achieve\nan acceptable level of Occupational Health & Safety performance. This document forms an\nintegral part of the Contract and Principal Contractors should make it part of any Contracts that\nthey may have with Contractors and/or Suppliers.\nCompliance with this document does not absolve the Principal Contractor and other Contractors\nfrom complying with minimum legal requirements. All Contractors remain responsible for the\nhealth & safety of his employees, persons other than his employees in terms of Section 9 of the\nOccupational Health and Safety Act, No. 85 of 1993 and those of his Mandatory’s\n1.3 General Occupational Health and Safety Provisions\n1.3.1 Hazard Identification & Risk Assessment\n1.3.1.1 Development of Risk Assessments\nEvery Contractor shall appoint a competent person in writing to perform a Risk Assessment before\nthe commencement of any Construction work. This Risk Assessment shall form part of the\nOccupational Health and Safety Plan and be implemented and maintained as contemplated in\nConstruction regulation 5(1).\nThe Risk Assessment shall include at least the following:\n• the identification of the risks and hazards to which persons may be exposed to\n• the analysis and evaluation of the identified risks and hazards\n• a documented plan of safe work procedures to mitigate, reduce or control the risks and\nhazards that have been identified\n• a monitoring plan, and\n• a review plans\n• material safety data sheets\nBased on the Risk Assessments, the Contractor must develop a set of site-specific Occupational\nHealth & Safety rules that will be applied to regulate the Occupational Health & Safety aspects of\n\nthe construction. The Risk Assessments, together with the site-specific Occupational Health &\nSafety rules shall be submitted to the Client before mobilisation on site commences.\nThe Contractor is required to conduct a baseline Risk Assessment of the risks he anticipates\nencountering during the project. The baseline Risk Assessment must include the Standard\nWorking Procedures (SWP) and the applicable Method Statements based on the Risk\nAssessments.\n1.3.1.2 Review of Risk Assessments\nThe Contractor is to review the Hazard Identification, Risk Assessments and Safe Work\nProcedure's at each Production Planning and Progress Report meeting as the Contract work\ndevelops and progresses and each time changes are made to the designs, plans and construction\nmethods and processes [monthly].\nThe Contractor shall provide the Client, other Contractors and all other concerned-parties with\ncopies of any changes, alterations or amendments brought about by the above.\n1.3.2 Legal Requirements\nAll Contractors entering into a Contract with the Client, shall, as a minimum, comply with the:\n• Occupational Health & Safety Act and Regulations (Act 85 of 1993). A current, up-to-date\ncopy of the Occupational Health Safety Act shall be available on site always.\n• Compensation for Occupational Injuries & Diseases Act (Act 130 of 1993). The Principal\nContractor will be required to submit a letter of Registration and "good-standing" from the\nCompensation Insurer before being awarded the Contract. A current, up-to-date copy of\nthe Compensation for Occupational Injury and Diseases Act (COIDA) shall be\navailable on site at all times.\n• The Client must determine the competency of Contractors/persons he allows (authorise) to\nenter such premises.\n1.3.3 Structure and Responsibilities\n1.3.3.1 Overall Supervision and Responsibility for Occupational Health and Safety\n- The Client is to ensure that the Contractor, appointed in terms of Construction Regulation\n5(1) (k), implements and maintains the agreed and approved Occupational Health & Safety\nPlan.\n- The Chief Executive Officer of the Contractor, in terms of Section 16(1) of the Act, is to\nensure that the Employer (as defined in the Act) complies with the Act. Annexure 5. "Audit\nSystem" may be used for this purpose.\n- It is a requirement that the Contractor, when he appoints Contractors in terms of\nConstruction Regulations 7 includes an Occupational Health & Safety Act Section 37(2)\nagreement (“Agreement with Mandatory”) in his agreement with such Contractors.\n- Every project must have an Occupational Health & Safety Act (85 /1993), Section 16(2)\nAppointee.\n- The client must ensure that the contractor appoints a Construction Supervisor and Assistant\nConstruction Supervisor in terms of Construction Regulation 8(8).\n1.3.3.2 Further (Specific) Supervision Responsibilities for Occupational Health & Safety\nThe Contractor shall appoint designated competent employees and/or other competent persons\nas required by the Act and Regulations. The appointments shall be in writing and the\nresponsibilities clearly stated together with the period for which the appointment is made. This\ninformation must be communicated and agreed with the appointees.\n\n1.3.3.3 Designation of Occupational Health & Safety Representatives (Section 18 of the\nOccupational Health & Safety Act)\nThe Contractors shall ensure Occupational Health & Safety Representatives are appointed for\nevery workplace where employees (including the employees of other Contractors) are exposed to\nrisk.\nOccupational Health & Safety Representatives have to be designated in writing and the\ndesignation must include the area of responsibility of the person and term of the designation.\nThe Contractor shall ensure that the designated OH&S Representatives conduct an inspection of\ntheir respective areas of responsibility using a checklist and report thereon.\nOccupational Health & Safety representatives shall be included in accident/incident investigations\nand must attend all Occupational Health & Safety committee meetings.\n1.3.4 Administrative Controls and the Occupational Health & Safety File\n1.3.4.1 The Occupational Health & Safety File\nAs required by Construction Regulation 7(1)(b), the Principal Contractor and other Contractors\nwill each keep an updated Occupational Health & Safety File on site containing the following\ndocuments as a minimum:\n• Permit to construct Notification of Construction Work (Construction Regulations 4)\n• Copy of Occupational Health & Safety Act (updated) (Gen Administrative Regulation 4)\n• Proof of Registration and good standing with a COID Insurer (Construction Regulation\n5(1) (j)\n• Occupational Health & Safety Programme/Plan agreed with the Client including the\nunderpinning Risk Assessment/s & Method Statements (Construction Reg 5(1)(q)\n• Copies of Occupational Health & Safety Committee and other relevant Minutes\n• Designs/drawings\n• A list of Contractors including copies of the agreements between the parties (Section\n37(2) agreement in terms of the OHS act) and the type of work being done by each\nContractor\n• Appointment/Designation forms (For example H&S rep, first aider etc.)\n• Electrical Installations, -Equipment & -Appliances including temporary certificate of\ncompliance\n• All other applicable records\n1.3.5 OH&S Goals & Objectives & Arrangements for Monitoring & Review of\nOccupational Health and Safety Performance\nThe Contractor is required to report all incidents to the Project Manager/Client. The Project\nmanager must also submit an up to date report regarding all incidents to the Head, OHS.\n1.3.6 Notification of Construction Work\nThe Contractors shall, where the Contract meets the requirements laid down in Construction\nRegulation 4, notify the Department of Labour at least 7 days before the commencement of work\nof the intention to carry out construction work.\nA copy must be held on the Occupational Health & Safety File and included into the project file.\n\n1.3.7 Training, Awareness and Competence\n1.3.7.1 General Induction Training\nAll persons on site are to attend a general induction session presented by the Contractor.\nAll persons on the site shall be in possession of documentation/proof that they have undergone\nGeneral Induction training.\nThe Contractor will be required to develop project specific induction training based on the Risk\nAssessments for the Contract work and train all employees and other Contractors and their\nemployees in this.\n1.3.7.2 Other Training\nAll operators, drivers and users of construction vehicles, mobile plant and other equipment (for\nexample overhead cranes) shall be in possession of documentation proving that they have\nundergone training to operate said vehicles, plant and equipment.\nAll employees in jobs requiring training in terms of the Act and Regulations shall be in possession\nof valid proof of training as required in the portfolio of evidence of the contractor.\n1.3.7.3 Awareness & Promotion\nThe Contractor is required to have scheme in place to promote an Occupational Health & Safety\nawareness and culture in employees. The following are some of the methods that may be used:\n• Toolbox Talks\n• Occupational Health & Safety Posters\n• Videos\n• Competitions\n• Suggestion schemes\n• Participative activities such as Occupational Health & Safety circles.\n1.3.7.4 Competence\nThe Contractor shall ensure that all appointed staff is competent and that all training required to\ndo the work safely and without risk to health, has been completed before work commences.\nThe Contractor shall ensure that follow-up and refresher training is conducted as the contract work\nprogresses and the work situation change. Records of all training shall be kept on the Health &\nSafety file for auditing purposes.\n1.3.8 Consultation, Communication and Liaison\nOccupational Health & Safety Liaison between the Client, Principal Contractor, other Contractors,\nDesigner and other concerned parties will be through the Client/Project Manager. In addition to\nthe above, communication may be directly with the Client or his appointed Agent, verbally or in\nwriting, as and when the need arises.\nThe Principal Contractor will be required to do Site Safety Audits with the Client/Project Manager\non a basis to be determined between the two parties.\n1.3.9 Checking, Reporting and Corrective Actions\n3.3.9.1 Monthly Audit by Client (Construction Regulation 4(1)(d)\nThe Client or his agent will conduct minimum monthly audits to comply with Construction\nRegulation 5(1) (o) to ensure that the Contractor has implemented and is maintaining the agreed\nand approved Occupational Health & Safety Plan.\n\nThe Contractor is to conduct his own minimum monthly internal audits to verify compliance with\nhis own Occupational Health & Safety plan.\nThe Occupational Health & Safety Representative is to conduct monthly inspections of their areas\nof responsibility and report thereon to their supervisor\nAll the results of the abovementioned inspections shall be in writing, reviewed, endorsed and\nplaced on the Occupational Health & Safety File.\n1.3.10 Incident Reporting and Investigation\n1.3.10.1 Reporting of Accidents and Incidents\nThe Contractor shall report all incidents where an employee is injured on duty to the extent that\nhe/she:\n• dies\n• becomes unconscious\n• loses a limb or part of a limb\n• is injured or becomes ill to such a degree that he/she is likely either to die or to suffer a\npermanent physical defect or likely to be unable for a period of at least 14 days either to\nwork or continue with the activity for which he/she was usually employed\nOR where:\n• a major incident occurred\n• the health or safety of any person was endangered\n• where a dangerous substance was spilled\n• the uncontrolled release of any substance under pressure took place\n• machinery or any part of machinery fractured or failed resulting in flying, falling or\nuncontrolled moving objects\n• machinery ran out of control\nto the Client and to the Provincial Director of the Department of Labour forthwith (Section 24 of\nthe Act & General Administrative Regulation 8.)\nThe Contractor is required to provide the Client with copies of all internal and external\naccident/incident investigation as well as all statutory reports required in terms of the Act within 7\ndays of the incident occurring.\n1.3.10.2 Accident and Incident Investigation\nThe Contractor is responsible for the investigation of all accidents/incidents where employees and\nnon-employees were injured to the extent that he/she/they had to be referred for medical\ntreatment by a doctor, hospital or clinic and the results of the investigation shall be entered into\nthe Accident/Incident Register.\nThe Contractor is responsible for the investigation of all minor, non-injury incidents and near\nmisses. The Client reserves the right to hold its own investigation into an incident or call for an\nindependent external investigation.\n1.3.11 Operational Control\n1.3.11.1 Emergency Preparedness, Contingency Planning and Response\nThe Contractor shall appoint a competent person to act as Emergency Coordinator.\nThe Contractor shall conduct an emergency identification exercise and establish what\nemergencies could possibly develop. He/she must then develop detailed contingency plans and\nemergency procedures.\n\n1.3.11.2 First Aid\nThe Contractor shall provide relevant First Aid equipment and have qualified First Aider/s on site\nas required by General Safety Regulation 3 of the Occupational Health & Safety Act.\n1.3.11.3 Security\nThe Contractor shall develop, implement and maintain Security- and Site Access Control rules\nand procedures throughout the construction period. Access control shall include the rule that non-\nemployees will not be allowed on site unaccompanied.\n1.3.11.4 Fall Protection (Working in Elevated Positions)\nAny work undertaken at height above ground level higher than two metres or any floor level will\nbe classified as "Work in Elevated Positions" and a pre-emptive Risk Assessment shall be carried\nout.\nWorkers working in elevated positions shall be trained to do this safely, without risk and compliant\nwith legislation.\nRisk Assessment shall take the possibility into account of persons falling through fragile material,\nskylights and other openings in the roof.\n1.3.11.5 Structures\nThe Contractor shall ensure that:\n• Steps are taken to ensure that no structure becomes unstable or collapses due to\nconstruction work being performed on it or in the vicinity of it\n• No structure is overloaded to the extent where it becomes unsafe\n• He/she has received from the designer the following information:\n• Information on known or anticipated hazards relating to the construction work and the\nrelevant information required for the safe execution of the construction work.\n• A geo-scientific report (where applicable)\n• The loading the structure is designed to bear\n• The methods and sequence of the construction process\n• All drawings pertaining to the design are on site and available for inspection\n1.3.11.6 Temporary Works\nTemporary work shall be carried out under the supervision of a competent person designated in\nwriting to do so.\nAll drawings pertaining to the temporary work shall be kept available on site. A competent person\nshall check all equipment used in the erection of temporary work before it is used.\n1.3.11.7 Access Scaffolding\nAccess Scaffolding shall be erected, used and maintained safely in accordance with Construction\nRegulation 16 and SA Bureau of Standards Code of Practice, SANS 085 entitled, "The Design,\nErection, Use & Inspection of Access Scaffolding.\nDetailed consideration shall be given to all scaffolding to ensure that it is properly planned to meet\nthe working requirements.\nScaffolding may only be erected, altered or dismantled by a person who has adequate training\nand experience in this type of work or under the supervision of such a person (Proof of\ncompetence to be put on the OHS File).\n1.3.11.8 Construction Vehicles & Mobile Plant (CV&MP)\nAll Construction Vehicles and Mobile Plant shall be inspected by the Contractor prior to being\nallowed on a project site and suppliers of hired vehicles, plant and equipment will be required to\ncomply with this specification as well as the Occupational Health & Safety Act and Regulations.\n\nNo unauthorised persons are to be allowed to drive CV&MP. Operators/drivers of CV&MP shall\nbe competent to operate the equipment safely and be in possession of a valid medical certificate\nissued by an Occupational Medicine Practitioner testifying that the holder is physically and\npsychologically fit to operate the equipment.\n1.3.11.9 Electrical Installations\nTemporary electrical installations shall be carried out by competent persons, and controlled by a\ncompetent person that has been appointed to do so in writing, in accordance with Construction\nRegulation 24 and the Electrical Installation Regulations. Temporary electrical installations shall\nbe inspected at least once per week by a competent person and a record of the inspections kept\nin the Occupational Health & Safety File.\nThe Contractor shall ensure that:\n• existing electrical services are located and marked before construction commences and\nduring the progress thereof. Where this is not possible, workers with jackhammers etc. are\nto be protected against electric shock by the use of suitable protective equipment like\ninsulated handles, rubber mats etc.\n• electrical installations and -machinery are sufficiently robust to withstand working conditions\non site.\n• all electrical machinery used on site are inspected before start-up on a daily basis by a\ncompetent person and that a record of the inspection is kept in the Occupational Health &\nSafety File.\nAn electrical and mechanical lock-out procedure for the construction site shall be developed by\nthe Principal Contractor and submitted for approval by the Project Manager before construction\ncommences. This lock-out procedure shall be adhered to by all Contractors on site.\n1.3.11.10 Housekeeping\nThe Contractor shall ensure that good housekeeping practises are implemented so that:\n• an unimpeded work space is maintained for every employee.\n• the walls and roof of every indoor workplace is sound and leak-free.\n• every workplace is kept clean, orderly and free of tools and materials that is not required\nfor the work being done.\n• every floor, walkway, stair, passage and gangway is kept in a good state of repair, skid-\nfree and free of obstruction, waste and materials.\n• catch platforms or -nets are erected over entry and exit ways or over places where\npersons are working to prevent them being struck by falling objects.\n• openings in floors, hatchways, stairways and open sides of floors or buildings are\nbarricaded, fenced, boarded over or provided with protection to prevent persons from\nfalling through or off them.\n• materials and equipment are stored properly.\n• materials ready for use is placed safely and not allowed to accumulate or cause an\nobstruction to pedestrian and vehicular traffic.\n• Scrap, waste and debris is removed regularly and in a safe manner.\n• construction sites are fenced off to prevent entry by unauthorised persons.\n\n1.3.11.11 Eating-, Changing-, Washing- and Toilet Facilities\nEating facilities should be provided in a location that is sheltered from the elements.\nAdequate changing-, washing – and toilet facilities shall be provided for both sexes.\nAt least 1 shower per 15 workers and 1 toilet per 30 workers shall be provided. Chemical toilets\nmay be used instead of the water borne sewerage type.\n1.3.11.12 Personal & Other Protective Equipment\nThe Contractor shall identify the hazards in the workplace and endeavour to eliminate them.\nWhere this is not possible, suitable steps shall be taken to protect workers from these hazards.\nEngineering- and other solutions to mitigate the hazard(s) should be attempted before the issue\nof personal protective equipment (PPE) is considered.\nThe Contractor is required to inform employees of health and safety hazards and issue them with\nsuitable equipment to protect them from these hazards. It is a further requirement that the\nContractor maintains the equipment and instructs and train employees in the use of the\nequipment. Employees do not have the right to refuse to use/wear safety equipment.\n1.3.11.13 Portable Electrical Tools & Equipment\nPortable electrical tools and equipment is defined as units taking electrical power from 220Volt 15\nAmp power outlets and is moved around the workplace to perform work like drilling, sawing,\ngrinding etc. and also include portable lights. Electrical appliances, on the other hand, include\nitems like fridges, stoves and heaters.\n1.3.11.14 Public Health & Safety\nThe Contractor is responsible for ensuring that non-employees affected by the construction work,\nlike visitors, the surrounding community and passers-by, are made aware of the dangers likely to\narise from the construction work as well as the precautionary measures to be observed to avoid\nor minimise these dangers. Appropriate signage must be posted to this effect and all employees\non site shall be instructed to ensure that non-employees are protected at all times. All non-\nemployees entering the site must receive induction into the hazards and risks and the control\nmeasures.\n\nAnnexure “A”\nCONSTRUCTION OCCUPATIONAL HEALTH - SAFETY – RISK ASSESSMENT\nDenotes items applicable to both Construction sites and Contractors Plant/Storage Yards\nELEMENT REMARKS\n1. Administrative & Legal Requirements • Dept. of labour will be notified – Annexure 2\n• Updated copy of OHS Act will be available on site\n• All legally required appointments will be made as specified in the OHS Act and\nConstruction Regulations\n• Site specific health and safety specification will be drawn up and provided to all\nprospective tendering contractors\n• Site specific risk assessment will be conducted and monitored and reviewed on\na regular basis\n• Written proof of registration and good standing of contractor with COID will be\nobtained\n• Health and safety committee will be established and meetings conducted\n• All contractors will be required to enter into a Section 37(2) agreement\n• All incidents/accidents will be reported and investigated as required\n• Detailed and site-specific fall protection plan will be drawn up and implemented\n– Employees fitness to work at heights will be determined and records kept\n• Cherry pickers will be load tested and valid load test certificates will be kept on\nfile – Regular safety inspections by competent persons done and records kept\n• Only persons medically tested in the form of Annexure 3 of the Construction\nRegulations and declared medically fit for the type of construction work to be\ndone will be allowed to work on site\n• All excavations will be inspected by a competent person before every shift as\nrequired, edges will be sloped to at least the angle of repose, the excavations\nwill be substantially barricaded and egress will be provided at least every 6\nmetres\n• Demolition work will be carried out under the supervision of a competent person,\ndetailed and site specific risk assessment will be carried out and engineering\nsurvey and method statement will be available on site\n\n• Inspections to prevent premature collapse will be carried out by competent\nperson before each shift. Inspection register kept\n• Cranes/Lifting Machines & equipment will be operated under the supervision of\na competent person\n• Valid load test certificates and 3 monthly inspection records of all lifting tackle\nwill be kept on site\n• Emergency and fire protection plan will be drawn up and displayed, emergency\nteams trained and available\n• All the legally required first aid equipment will be provided and clearly located -\nTrained and qualified first aiders available on site\n• Assessment will be conducted to determine the personal protective equipment\nrequirements, all equipment issued free of charge and the wearing of the\nequipment will be strictly enforced\n• Gas welding/cutting equipment only used by competent persons and equipment\nplaced on register and inspected regularly to ensure its safety\n• Alphabetical list of all chemicals on site will be drawn up and material safety\ndata sheets for all hazardous substances obtained – first aiders will be trained in\nthe correct first aid measures to be taken in case of injury or illness caused by\nhazardous chemicals\n• All construction vehicles will be inspected daily before start up and only operated\nby operators who are competent and medically fit to do so\n1. Education, Training & Promotion • All employees entering the site will be required to undergo a site specific\nhealth and safety induction training programme – A copy of the site rules\nwill also be made available to them\n• Relevant employees will receive specific safety training such a training in\nthe safe work procedures for plant, equipment and substances they are\nrequired to use\n• All visitors to the site will be given induction training and will only be\nallowed on the site if they are accompanied by a member of the site staff\n• Specific training will include inter alia first aid training, general safety\ntraining, firefighting training, operator training\n\n1. Public Safety & Emergency • Signage will be used to limit access to the site – “No unauthorised entry”,\n“Visitors to report to site office” and other relevant signage will be used\nPreparedness\n• General signage warning of overhead work and other hazards on site will be\ndeployed\n• Netting or other measures will be used to protect persons from falling\nobjects\n• Security measures such as patrols to prevent unauthorised entry as well as\nan entry register will placed in use\n1. Personal Protective Equipment • Assessment will be conducted to determine the personal protective equipment\nrequired on site\n• All equipment will be issued free of charge and the wearing thereof strictly\nenforced – this will also count for visitors to the site\n1. Housekeeping • Removal of rubble will form part of the project – Rubble to be crushed and\nremoved by truck to predetermined dump site\n• High standards of housekeeping will be enforced on all contractors\n1. Scaffolding, Formwork & Support work All legal requirements to be addressed and adhered to\n2. Ladders All legal requirements to be addressed and adhered to\n3. Electrical Safeguarding All legal requirements to be addressed and adhered to\n4. Emergency/Fire Prevention & Protection • Sufficient firefighting equipment will be provided, correctly located and clearly\nsignposted\n• Emergency plan will be formulated for evacuation and published\n1. Excavations & Demolition • Demolition work will be carried out under the supervision of a competent person,\ndetailed and site specific risk assessment will be carried out and engineering\nsurvey and method statement will be available on site\n• Inspections to prevent premature collapse will be carried out by competent person\nbefore each shift. Inspection register kept\n• All excavations will be inspected by a competent person before every shift as\nrequired, edges will be sloped to at least the angle of repose, the excavations well\nbe substantially barricaded and egress will be provided at least every 6 metres\n\n• All excavations will be inspected by a competent person before every shift as\nrequired, edges will be sloped to at least the angle of repose, the excavations will\nbe substantially barricaded and egress will be provided at least every 6 metres\n1. Tools • All hand tools will be in good condition and will be inspected regularly for safety –\nFindings will be entered into a register kept for this purpose\n1. Cranes • Cranes/Lifting Machines & equipment will be operated under the supervision of a\ncompetent person\n• Valid load test certificates and 3 monthly inspection records of all lifting tackle will\nbe kept on site\n1. Personnel & Material Hoists • Cherry pickers will be load tested and valid load test certificates will be kept on file\n– Regular safety inspections by competent persons done and records kept – Only\npersons medically tested for physical and psychological fitness and declared\ncompetent will be allowed to work on cherry pickers\n1. Transport & Materials Handling • All construction vehicles will be inspected daily before start up and only operated\nby operators who are competent and medically fit to do so\n1. Site Plant & Machinery • All construction vehicles will be inspected daily before start up and only operated\nby operators who are competent and medically fit to do so\n1. Plant & Storage Yards/Site Workshops Specifics • Good housekeeping practices and environmental protection to be practiced as far\nas is reasonably practicable\n1. Health & Hygiene • All hygiene facilities such as toilets, eating areas, change rooms and the like will\nbe provided in line with the Facilities Regulations and the Construction\nRegulations and will be kept in a clean and hygienic condition\n\n1. Soil profile summaries (all depths in m). 36\n© 2021-07-29 KHg Applied Geologists Page iv\n\nReport 121-022-1: Phase 2 Engineering Geological Investigation: Rosedale 400\n1. INTRODUCTION\n1.1. Terms of Reference\nAn Engineering Geological Investigation was requested by the Client for the proposed development of Rosedale 400 in the\nNorthern Cape Province. All localities and maps are based on information supplied by and agreed on with the Client.\nThe proposed development comprising residential developments with the associated infrastructure such as roads and buried\npipelines. This is the Phase 2 Engineering Geological/ Geotechnical Report that in which the outcome is to indicate geotechnical\nzones for the individual stands on the site drawings supplied by the client.\nThe investigation was conducted in accordance with the accepted proposal, as well as the scope of works and literature\nreferences provided in this document. Field work and reporting were conducted and/ or overseen by professionally registered\nscientists with the South African Council for Natural Scientific Professions.\n1.2. Scope\nThe Engineering Geological Investigation comprises the following as per draft SANS 634:2012 (Geotechnical Investigations for\nHousing Developments) and the GFSH-2 (Generic Specification for Housing Developments):\n• Identify potential hazards\n• Define ground conditions (e.g. soil profile description, laboratory testing, inferring of hydrological conditions, etc.)\n• Classify land to be developed in terms of appropriate site class designations (assumed to be single-storey or double-\nstorey masonry buildings, unless specifically stated otherwise)\n• Provide appropriate recommendations with respect to further work and/ or, where appropriate, land use planning,\nmitigation measures, and/ or risk management.\n• Confirm and refine the site class designation for each individual stand (Phase 2).\nThe scope of work excludes the following investigations:\n• Dolomite stability, or historical mining and undermining investigations\n• Detailed hydrological, hydropedological, hydrogeological, flood line, or wetland delineation studies\n• Geophysical, resistivity, or corrosion studies\n• Surveying and associated studies\n• Detailed design or specialist studies not within the accepted scope of works.\n1.3. Desktop Information Sources\nIf historical data in the form of measurements, laboratory results, reports, and so forth are available, these are incorporated to\ncompile a single updated record of data for a site. Such data usually fall under widespread ownership with various copyright and\nconfidentiality issues and are also very not often in the public domain. Specific literature and data sources are shown in Table 1.\n© 2021-07-29 KHg Applied Geologists Page 1\n\nReport 121-022-1: Phase 2 Engineering Geological Investigation: Rosedale 400\nTable 1. Glossary and Explanations\nClimate governs the moisture supply to the surface and subsurface, and subsequently the balance between infiltration and\nrunoff, and interflow and deep percolation (potential groundwater recharge). The N-value (Weinert 1980) is calculated as 12\ntimes the January evaporation divided by the annual precipitation and indicates whether an area is predominantly moisture-\nsurplus (N < 5) or moisture-deficit (N > 5). Moisture-surplus areas are more prone to chemical weathering, whereas moisture-\ndeficit areas are more prone to physical weathering. It is, however, still very likely that weathering and subsequent soil genesis\nincorporate both these types of weathering.\nThe phreatic zone refers the saturated zone which can serve as an aquifer. The vadose zone refers to the earth materials\nbetween the land surface and groundwater table, aquifer, and/ or saturated (phreatic) zone. The phreatic zone can be\nunconfined, implying connection to the atmosphere, of confined or leaky, implying lower permeability strata separating it from\nthe land surface. The vadose zone dictates movement of water between land surface and groundwater. This affects the\nrelationship between runoff and recharge, but also the possible movement of contaminants.\nSubsurface moisture can be stored for long periods of times, and flow is often lateral at shallow depths. The interpretation of\nground successions, often further supported by specialist hydropedologist investigations, provide insight into anticipating how\nmoisture movement and moisture contents varies over space and time. Through this, one can start to interpret the effect of\nshallow subsurface moisture during project life cycle.\nBedrock refers to the rock directly underlying the site and, when exposed to surface, is referred to as outcrop. Bedrock can be\nunderlain by other rock types at shallow depths, and it is usually overlain by residual soils derived of its in-situ weathering and\ntransported soils deposited from other positions. The regional geology provides geological context to the anticipated distribution\nof geological material and how these can affect the distribution of lithologies (rock types) with different chemical, mineralogical,\nstructural, and mechanical properties.\nSoil profiles are described based on origin, typically with transported materials overlying in-situ materials. Although some\nhorizons may be absent, the general succession is: transported (colluvium, alluvium, aeolian, hillwash, talus, etc., and as made\nground or fill when anthropogenically reworked); pebble marker; residual, completely weathered, highly weathered,\nmoderately weathered, slightly weathered, and fresh bedrock. A pebble marker – although not always distinctly identifiable –\ncommonly indicates the boundary between transported and residual (in-situ formed) soils.\nPedocretes, indicated by the presence of cementation, forms through periodic waterlogging of subsurface soils when mobile ions\nare mobilised and is followed by precipitation in drier periods. This indicates early stages with hardpan stages the near-final\nstages of formation, at which stage excavation by means of TLB become very difficult. Early stages of formation are evident is\n© 2021-07-29 KHg Applied Geologists Page 2\n\nReport 121-022-1: Phase 2 Engineering Geological Investigation: Rosedale 400\ndistinct discolouration of soil horizons, followed later by increased distinct mottling, nodules and concretions, well-cemented\nhoneycombs, and hardpans. Classifications is based on dominant cement, e.g., iron (iron-rich, ferruginized, ferricrete), calcium\n(calcic, calcified, calcrete), silica (siliceous, silcrete), gypsym (gypseous, gypcrete).\nMade ground is used collectively for any disturbed (i.e. loosened, moved, stockpiled, compacted, etc.) natural or imported\nmaterial. Manmade materials are often associated with such made ground (i.e. building rubble, rubbish and waste, geotextiles,\nsteel, plastics, etc.). Fill and rubble are used specifically for material apparently imported for the purpose of construction, or\nmaterial remnant of historical demolished construction.\n1.5. Cape Town.\n© 2021-07-29 KHg Applied Geologists Page 3\n\nReport 121-022-1: Phase 2 Engineering Geological Investigation: Rosedale 400\n1. SITE DESCRIPTION\n2.1. Locality and Prevailing Conditions\nThe property, Rosedale 400, covers an approximate extent of 25 hectares. It extends in the northwest beyond the existing formal\ndevelopment. The property is accessible from various directions by means of paths and roads (Figure 1).\nMajority of the site is presently already developed with mostly single-storey dwellings. Historical activities (such as agricultural\npractices, cut-to-fill operations, previous development) disrupted the surface materials and resulted in some made materials\nbeing present on the site.\n2.2. Climate and Vegetation\nThe greater Upington area is considered a desert climate and is classified as BWh by the Köppen and Geiger system. The area has\nan average annual temperature of 21.6 °C and an average annual rainfall of 219 mm with both temperature and rainfall being\nhighest during the summer months. The area is within the Nama-Karoo Biome very near to the northern border of the\nBushmanland Arid Grassland (NKb) comprising intermingling units of Lower Gariep Broken Veld, Kalahari Karroid Shrubland and\nGordonia Duneveld.\nThe climatic N-value is well above 5, implying that the area is moisture-deficit, and that physical weathering (disintegration) will\nlikely be more evident that chemical weathering (decomposition).\n2.3. Regional Geology\nThe site is underlain by (Figure 2):\n• Jannelsepan Formation (previously the Donkieboud Granite Gneiss, Mdo); pyroxene and massive amphibolite;\nhornblende-biotite, biotite and quartzo-feldspathic gneiss; calc-silicate rocks\nNo mineral deposits or significant geological features (faults, folds, igneous intrusions, etc.) are indicated on or near the site.\nThe site is not underlain by soluble rock (e.g., dolomite) and no specialist dolomite stability investigations are required.\n2.4. Topography and Regional Hydrology\nTopography is based on elevation profiles from Google Earth © and basic field observations. This does not substitute land survey\ndata. Topography and regional hydrology (e.g. steep slopes, very flat slopes, defined drainage features, and rapid changes in\nslope) affect the distribution of ground as it affects the weathering, erosion, transport, and deposition of materials. As such, it\nprovides a valuable indication of possible changes in subsurface ground and water conditions, but also affects the stability of\nslopes and the direction of surface runoff.\nThe site has a shallow variable slope of roughly 5% (Figure 3). The area is just southwest of the divide between the D73F (in which\nit falls) and D73E quaternary catchments (Figure 4) and likely drains from higher areas in the northwest through the D73F\nquaternary catchment into the Orange River that flow westward roughly 4 km south of the site.\nNo defined drainage features apart from this appear to be on the investigated part of the site, but initial seeps that confluence\nin the west can be inferred from Google Earth © imagery.\nPrecipitation is expected to flow as sheet flow or shallow interflow towards shallower slopes, natural depressions, and drainage\nfeatures from where infiltration can occur. Seasonal perching of groundwater may cause seepage or wet profile conditions at\nshallow depths, regardless of the extent of the 1:100-year flood lines.\n© 2021-07-29 KHg Applied Geologists Page 4\n\nReport 121-022-1: Phase 2 Engineering Geological Investigation: Rosedale 400\n2.5. Hydrogeology and Hydrostratigraphy\nThe groundwater occurs in intergranular and fractures aquifers of acid intrusive igneous rocks (granitoids) and with median\nborehole yields of 0.1-0.5 l/s (excluding dry boreholes). Electrical conductivity values are 70-300 mS/m.\nThe groundwater is of the (Ca,Mg)Cl - (Ca,Mg)SO - (Na,K)Cl - (Na,K) SO hydrochemical types. Total dissolved solids are 500-\n2 4 2 4\n1500 mg/l.\nGroundwater levels are 30-50 m with recharge rates of 1-10 mm per year.\nThe bedrock may act as a lower-permeability material with an irregular bedrock interface causing limited deep percolation or\ninfiltrated water and upward perching or seepage of ponded. This is a function of the material distribution and, although not\ninferred to occur given the climatic conditions of the area, it is implied to be more likely to evaporation and lateral movement of\nmoisture in the very shallow horizons, very low local deep percolation and groundwater recharge, and induced surface runoff in\nintensive rainfall events.\n© 2021-07-29 KHg Applied Geologists Page 5\n\nReport 121-022-1: Phase 2 Engineering Geological Investigation: Rosedale 400\n1. METHOD OF INVESTGATION\nAll maps are indicated in APPENDIX A. Coordinates were retrieved by means of handheld Garmin GPS and are displayed on\nGoogle Earth © imagery.\nForty-six (46) test pits were described from existing service trenches and TLB excavations on 5 May2021 (Figure 5). Shattered hyphenated,\nVery moist discolouration (Very) soft, firm, Slickensided roughly equal\nWet based on extent (very) stiff Relic structures parts\nORIGIN STRENGTH COLOUR STRUCTURE TEXTURE OTHER\nHighly weathered Soft-rock Light grey to Foliated to Generally coarse- Amphibolite\nJannelsepan becoming white streaked closely jointed grained Gneiss and Calc-\nFormation medium-hard rock black, olive Silicate\n(Extremely to very) As for soils Very thick; Thick; Petrological: Grain size;\nweak; (medium to Medium; Thin; Phaneritic; Ophilitic; mineralogy; etc.\nextremely) strong Very thin Crystalline\nThickly/ narrow Porphyrytic;\nThinly/ very Amorphous; etc.\nnarrow\nBedrock is highly variable, comprising amphibolite gneiss and calc-silicates of the Jannelsepan Formation. Residual and\ncompletely weathered horizons are calcified grading into nodular calcrete with depth, and finally into hardpan calcrete on the\nbedrock interface in most of the profiles.\nMost shallow horizons are silty sands with variable amounts of gravels derived from the proximate country rock and calcrete.\nThe consistencies and structures as described are generally highly variable with loose voided pockets occurring throughout. With\ndepth, induration increases due to the calcification of the profile, becoming harder with depth.\nThe bedrock surface is highly variable across the area. Hardpan calcrete forming on the bedrock also varies greatly in thickness,\nextent, and continuity, but generally seems to form above the less-weathered bedrock. Variable bedrock topography and\nlithology (rock type) may influence excavatability and residual soil properties over small distances.\nHistorical development, agricultural operations and/ or levelling practices may have disrupted the surficial materials and\nvariations in soil properties should be accounted for. The likelihood of imported fill and building rubble should also be accounted\nfor in shallow horizons.\nFill was identified on the site and constitutes highly variable material with very inconsistent properties.\n© 2021-07-29 KHg Applied Geologists Page 7\n\nReport 121-022-1: Phase 2 Engineering Geological Investigation: Rosedale 400\nTable 4. Rosedale 400\n4.2. Excavatability\nProfiles were described in service trenches excavated prior to field work, many of which were already partially backfilled, and in\nadditional test pits excavated by means of TLB.\nTo account for the different types of exposures, depth of soft excavation was inferred based on the depth to hardpan calcrete or\nto highly or moderately weathered bedrock (Table 4). Figure 5 shows the depth of soft excavation for the profiles.\nCalcrete and bedrock occur are variable depths across the site.\nExcavation conditions are SOFT in transported to nodular calcrete horizons. Conditions become HARD in hardpan ferricrete, and\nMEDIUM-HARD in weathered bedrock (SANS 1200D:1988 and SANS 634:2012).\n4.3. Stability and Water\nProfiles were described in exposed trenches and test pits exposed during the field work. Sidewall instabilities and water seepage\nwere not encountered in any of the test pits during this period.\nDiscolouration (speckles, mottles and/ or patches) and calcrete in all horizons and at depth in most profiles suggest the likely\npresence of a localised perched water table. These systems result from soils periodically reaching saturation from infiltrating\nwater, shallow interflow or rising subsurface waters.\nNo additional groundwater information was acquired during the site investigation.\n4.4. Laboratory Results\nThe sampled horizons are indicated in Table 2 and the full laboratory results are shown in APPENDIX D.\nTen (10) samples submitted for foundation indicator testing (Table 5). All tested materials grade as non-plastic, non-expansive\nsand and gravel, with the transported soils being sandier and the weathered amphibole gneiss being gravellier.\nTable 5. ENGINEERING GEOLOGICAL EVALUATION\n5.1. Impacts for Urban Development\nThe impact of the geotechnical constraints on housing developments can be evaluated according to the general geotechnical\nconstraints relevant to urban development shown in Table 7 and APPENDIX F (Partridge et al. 1993). The descriptor indicates the\nseverity of the specific constraint for this site and, where multiple values are indicated, its variation across the site. For non-\nresidential developments, the system is applied as a sensible means of denoting geological constraints and should not be viewed\nas a foundation design recommendation as the structure’s footprint and load will determine this.\nTable 7. n/a\nor channel with slope < 1% channel or floodplain\nNOTES: bold text and grey-shaded cells indicate site-specific conditions based on field and laboratory data.\n* asterisk denotes localised occurrences, or information outside the scope of this investigation.\n5.2. Problem Soil Behaviour and Volume Change\nPossible problem soil behaviour and volume change relate to A, C and D (settlement, collapse, expansiveness, and\ncompressibility) as per Table 7.\nBulk of the site materials, given both its soil mass properties and thickness, will likely be prone to settlement consolidation. No\nexpansive behaviour is expected given the very low clay contents and plasticity indices.\n5.3. Subsidence and Sinkhole Formation\nUndermining and dolomitic land can result in subsidence or sinkhole formation as per G and H in Table 7.\n© 2021-07-29 KHg Applied Geologists Page 11\n\nReport 121-022-1: Phase 2 Engineering Geological Investigation: Rosedale 400\nNo historical or current mining are present on the site. Although it is not anticipated, investigations of the effects of undermining\nare not within the scope of this investigation.\nCalc-silicates and calcrete do constitute soluble rock. They do, however, not constitute the risks necessitating a dolomite stability\ninvestigation, and no specialist dolomite investigation is required.\n5.4. Water and Drainage\nPossible seepage and water-related issues relate to B and L of Table 7. Additional to this are any possible contamination from the\nsite or influences of water and its chemistry on buried infrastructure.\nMoisture conditions never exceeded the ‘slightly moist’ descriptor, implying site soils were not near saturation or field capacity\nduring the time of investigation. Inspection was during the dry season.\nMottling and calcification in all test pits indicate the presence of seasonal to ephemeral wetness, typically within the residual\nhorizons and completely weathered horizons. This is likely to be due to infiltrated water that evaporates, concentrating the\ncalcium compounds as nodules and concretions as it moves down, and perching on the bedrock surface (and later the hardpan\ncalcrete surface itself), causing increased induration with depth.\nRainfall events are sparse but intense, affecting the balance between runoff and infiltration. Already shallow slopes with limited\nvegetation may result in runoff and limited infiltration.\nScaling and corrosion of buried infrastructure generally increases with increasing electrical conductivity, pH extremes, excessive\nclay minerals, and partial saturation (Table 8). Additional to this, chloride, sulphate, pH, organic matter, and other contaminants\nalso have significant influences. Site soils are granular with low clay contents, moderate electrical conductivity (with one sample\nbeing very high) and slightly alkaline pH-values. The determination of EC and pH is done on only the fine fraction that makes up\nless than 10% of the site soils. Moisture contents are low throughout, and the calcification of the site soils can buffer corrosion\nnotably against steel. Moderate corrosion should be considered, regardless, notably in the event of changing the properties of\nthe site soils.\nTable 8. General influences of the corrosiveness of soils to steel and concrete.\nPARAMETER à INCREASING AGGRESIVENESS OR CORROSIVENESS OF SOILS à\nEC (S/m) <0.0050: <0.0100: <0.0200: <0.0333: <0.1000: >0.1000:\nEssentially Mildly Moderately Corrosive Highly Extremely\nnon-corrosive corrosive corrosive corrosive corrosive\nSoil type Sand and gravel generally have lower EC: Clay generally has higher EC:\ngenerally less corrosive generally more corrosive\nSaturation <40% or 100%: generally not 60-85%: maximum expected\naggressive to mildly corrosive corrosion rates\npH Soils with neutral pH are generally not very Very acidic or alkaline: corrosive to steel\ncorrosive Acidic (pH<5.5): likely corrosive to concrete\nNOTES: bold text and grey-shaded cells indicate site-specific conditions based on field and laboratory data.\n* asterisk denotes localised occurrences.\nSignificant changes in moisture content may contribute to the anticipated movement behaviour of the site soils. During\nconstruction and after development, shallow perched water systems may develop yet further due to stormwater management\npractices and localised infiltration. The complete development requires plumbing and drainage precautions. The installation of\ndrains may prevent super-saturation of the site soils and minimise differential movements on surface level.\nFlood line determination, wetland delineation, hydropedological assessments, and hydrogeological investigations do not fall\nwithin the scope of this investigation.\n5.5. Excavation and Stability\nExcavation and stability issues relate to E, F, I, J and K (erodibility, excavatability, slopes, seismicity) of Table 7 and is judged\ntogether with other parameters influencing stability of cuttings, excavations, slopes, and the like.\n© 2021-07-29 KHg Applied Geologists Page 12\n\nReport 121-022-1: Phase 2 Engineering Geological Investigation: Rosedale 400\nBedrock comprises calc-silicates and various closely jointed gneisses overlying by nodular to hardpan calcrete throughout. Soft\nexcavation is possible in the transported soils and the nodular calcrete (inferred to be in the residual and completely weathered\nhorizons) to depths exceeding 0.5 m across the site.\nDeeper excavation conditions and fresh bedrock properties should be confirmed for excavation depths exceeding the depth of\ninvestigation (such as cut to fill, basements and underground storage tanks).\nExcavation instabilities may be encountered, especially in waterlogged soils or in excavations left open for prolonged periods.\nSite slopes appear to be below 5%. Predominantly shallow site slopes and coarsely graded soils suggest low soil erodibility (Table\n9). Other factors, such as sparse vegetation and intense (albeit low frequency and low volume) rainfall events may, however,\ninduce erosion of the surface materials.\nTable 9. SITE CHARACTERISATION AND FINDINGS\n6.1. Material Properties and Uses\nThe usability of the on-site materials for construction purposes (based on the Unified classes) are shown in APPENDIX G and the\nusability of the on-site material for sub-grade, sub-base and base layers (based on the AASHO and Unified Soil Classification\nSystems) are shown in Table 10.\nTable 10. AASHO and Unified rating for sub-grade, sub-base and base (from Dyer, 1982).\nSUB-GRADE SUB-BASE BASE\nVery poor Poor Fair Medium Good Medium Good Medium Good Excellent\nAASHTO SOIL CLASSIFICATION\nA-1-b A-1-a\nA-2-7 A-2-6 A-2-5 A-2-4\nA-3\nA-4\nA-5\nA-6\nA-7-6 A-7-5\nUNIFIED SOIL CLASSIFICATION\nOH CH GM-u GM-d\nMH OL GC\nCL SW\nML SM-d\nSC\nSM-u GP\nSP\nNOTES: bold text and grey-shaded cells indicate site-specific conditions based on field and laboratory data.\nBulk of the site materials will be suitable for use in the sub-base and base course layers for roads. Calcrete may become slippery\nwhen wet or generate dust when dry.\nOn-site materials will require sieving (to required grading) and/ or chemical stabilisation for use as bedding, fill or constructed fill\nareas.\nHistorical agricultural, levelling and cut-and-fill operations and associated made ground alter the properties of the ground and\naffect its behaviour. The same applies to soils with abundant organic materials.\n6.2. Site Class Designations and Geotechnical Zoning\nThe site is considered one site class designation zone based on the above constraints and the criteria as set out in the NHBRC\n(1999) guideline for single-story masonry buildings (APPENDIX F). The zones are shown in Figure 6 and summarised in Table 11.\nTable 11. Engineering geological/ geotechnical zoning.\nZONE GENERAL GEOTECHNICAL CONCERNS CLASS\nA Excavation mostly possible to depths exceeding 0.50 m but high variations likely; expected S1-R/2BDEF\nperiodical wetness above bedrock surface; soils prone to compression and consolidation;\nupper horizons likely erodible\nThe classification and foundation recommendations are based on results from this investigation and for present conditions.\nBoundaries for zones are inferred based on anticipated behaviour between sampled point and may vary.\n© 2021-07-29 KHg Applied Geologists Page 14\n\nReport 121-022-1: Phase 2 Engineering Geological Investigation: Rosedale 400\n6.3. Foundation Recommendations and Solutions\nFoundation recommendations are based on the site classes supplied in Figure 6. The proposed development will alter present\nconditions as investigated during complete project life cycle. The classification and foundation recommendations are based on\nresults from this investigation and for present conditions.\nFor heavier structures, improved foundations should be considered. For any required filling and levelling, proper foundation\ndesign will be required to minimise differential movement and to ensure suitable founding. Rippability of deeper materials may\nrequire further investigation, depending on the depth of construction.\nFor any excavations or buried structures such as pipelines or tank pits, appropriate excavation will be required if it exceeds the\ndepth of this investigation.\nHistorical development, agricultural operations and/ or levelling practices may have disrupted the surficial materials and\nvariations in soil properties should be accounted for. The likelihood of imported fill and building rubble should also be accounted\nfor in shallow horizons.\nSurface materials will be disturbed to an uncertain depth. The degree to which onsite materials are reworked and mixed\n(disturbed, compacted, mixed, stabilised) will affect consolidation settlement and other possible volume changes. Soil horizons\nwill inevitably be disrupted, thereby also affecting the behaviour of the ground profile. Compaction and improvement techniques\nemployed should be conducted with diligence in this regard.\nDisruption or removal of the shallow soil horizons will expose highly weathered to fresher bedrock which will fundamentally alter\nthe subsurface hydrological conditions, resulting in deeper percolation of water, changes in depth of the perched water table\nsystem, or surface ponding and runoff. The expected replacement of these upper horizons with material of better grading and\nlower plasticity will create new flow systems in anthropogenic materials with better drainage and higher permeability. Possible\nleaks in underground pipes may contribute to this interflow system. Design of a shallow subsurface drainage system should\nconsider this as water may adversely affect infrastructure (foundations and pavements) after construction.\nSurface runoff can be removed or allowed to infiltrate through permeable pavements to limit possible damage from sealed\nsurfaces. This can be exacerbated by facilities adding additional water to the development such as rainwater, irrigation, and\noutside watering and washing facilities.\nDrainage precautions and water management are required to minimise differential movements and erosion. Waterlogging,\ndevelopment of perched water or interflow systems, induced deep percolation, or other changes to the shallow subsurface\nmovement of water in relation to surface runoff will result due to changes to the current ground and water conditions. This may\ncause problems in, for instance, dampness in surface structures and with installation of services, flooding of underground\nopenings (basements, tank pits, graves) or excavations, additional collapse settlements or heave, and increased corrosivity to\nsteel or concrete.\nIf the site or a portion thereof is situated within the 1:100-year flood lines, or have been delineated as a wetland, it is the\nprerogative of the Civil Engineer or other suitably experienced specialist to overwrite the geotechnical recommendations for such\nportions.\nA suitably qualified civil engineer should approve design of foundations. Any levelled land should be constructed as homogeneous\nas possible to limit differential movements and to minimise adverse impacts on shallow interflow.\n© 2021-07-29 KHg Applied Geologists Page 15\n\nReport 121-022-1: Phase 2 Engineering Geological Investigation: Rosedale 400\n1. SUMMARY AND RECOMMENDATIONS\nThis report details the Phase 2 Engineering Geological (Geotechnical) Investigation for the residential development of Rosedal\n400 (Northern Cape).\nThe site is underlain by amphibolite gneiss and calc-silicate rocks of the Jannelsepan Formation. The transported horizons are\ngenerally shallow, becoming indurated with nodular calcrete in the residual horizons and eventually hardpan calcrete above the\nlesser-weathered bedrock.\nCalcification implies periodical changes in moisture and oxidation states of the site soils.\nSite soils are granular with no to very low plasticity indices and an overall voided structure. Consistency and structure increase\nwith depth. Soft excavation seems to generally be possible to depths exceeding 0.50 m across the site.\nVarious transported material covers the site and comprise silty sand with large amounts of different concretions, gravels and fine\ncobbles. The pebble marker is often dispersed into the transported soils.\nResidual to completely weathered amphibolite gneiss and calc-silicates are calcified into nodular calcrete becoming progressively\nmore indurated to hardpan calcrete on the lesser-weathered bedrock interface. Periodical seepage is a direct cause of this,\nimplying that shallow site drainage might be affected.\nThe site is considered one geotechnical zone. Major constraints include variable degrees of consolidation or settlement,\nassociated with seepage and minor heave. Wetlands delineated prior to this investigation form Zone III. Provisional foundation\nrequirements for single-storey masonry structures are as per Figure 6 (APPENDIX F), with individual zones supplied per stand in\nError! Reference source not found. (APPENDIX F).\n© 2021-07-29 KHg Applied Geologists Page 16\n\nReport 121-022-1: Phase 2 Engineering Geological Investigation: Rosedale 400\n1. REPORT PROVISIONS\n• All findings and recommendations are based solely on the data contained as detailed in this document and are subject to\nspatial and temporal variation.\n• Findings and recommendations contained in this report are subject to the representativeness of the excavations and/ or\noutcrops inspected.\n• The Client is responsible to provide the correct and appropriate information related to the site and the proposed type of\ndevelopment under investigation.\n• Although no doubt is placed over the quality of data obtained from other sources, the findings of this report are also subject\nto the accuracy of the results received from laboratories and the reliability of maps, and data from the Internet (including,\nfor instance, the DWA National Groundwater Archive, Google Earth, old reports, geological and topocadastral maps).\n• The test pits were positioned to cover the accessible parts to zone the site. The pits were backfilled by the TLB or excavator\nwithout proper compaction in layers. If structures are to be positioned over or across these pits proper compaction must be\nexecuted to prevent differential settlements from taking place. The same will apply to development across existing\nfoundations, waste pits, root areas of removed medium to large trees and septic tanks. Backfill of test pits excavated by the\nClient cannot be guaranteed. 0832795027\n• Broad reference to regional drainage and topography do not represent surveyed and investigated information, and specialist\nfindings in these studies override those in this report.\n• The 1:100-year flood lines, hydropedology and hydrogeology are not within the scope of this investigation.\n• A professional engineer must approve all designs.\n• It is assumed that the development will be serviced by the usual municipal services and no recommendations are made on\non-site sanitation, waste disposal, and stormwater reticulation services.\n• Additionally, refer to the Terms and Conditions of appointment as supplied by KHg Applied Geologists (trading as Moonlight\nEnviro (Pty) Ltd) and/ or Moonlight Enviro (Pty) Ltd in the accepted cost proposal(s).\n© 2021-07-29 KHg Applied Geologists Page 17\n\nReport 121-022-1: Phase 2 Engineering Geological Investigation: Rosedale 400\nAPPENDIX A. NC/15/2026\nTHE CONSTRUCTION OF 12 BNG HOUSES IN ROSEDALE 400, DAWID KRUIPER\nLOCAL MUNICIPALITY FOR THE ZF MGCAWU DISTRICT MUNICIPALITY\nC 4: SITE INFORMATION\nC 4.1 SCOPE\nThe documentation included describes the site as at the time of tender to enable the Tenderer to price\nhis tender and to decide upon his method of working and programming.\nWork will be executed in a residential area and the Contractor will take all necessary steps to ensure\nthe safety of people, animals and/or property.\nC 4.2 SUBSOIL INVESTIGATIONS, AND TEST RESULTS\nThe material on site varies. Soil on site is classified as C1/S/R and medium raft foundation is\nrecommended\nC 4.3 INFORMATION ABOUT PIPED AND OTHER SERVICES BELOW THE SURFACE OF THE SITE\nFOR CONTRACTS INVOLVING GROUND WORKS, AND ABOUT HOOK-UP AND BOUNDARY\nDETAILS FOR CONTRACTS WITH PLANT INTERFACES, IN ADDITION TO ANYTHING ABOUT\nTHE PHYSICAL SITE WHICH IMPACTS UPON THE CONTRACT\nAll existing services that could be indicated by the Employer are shown on the Drawings. The\nContractor will however investigate on site with the Engineer to identify any existing services that are\nnot indicated on the Drawings before any work commences in an area. As build drawings for sewer,\nwater, roads and electricity will be issued on commencement of the project\nPart C 4: Site Information C 4 - 1 C 4.1 - C 4.3\nTender Number: NC/15/2026\n\nVOLUME 2\n\nDRAWINGS\n\nDEPARTMENT OF CO-OPERATIVE GOVERNANCE, HUMAN SETTLEMENTS AND\nTRADITIONAL AFFAIRS OF THE NORTHERN CAPE\nTENDER NO. NC/15/2026\nTHE CONSTRUCTION OF 12 BNG HOUSES IN ROSEDALE 400, DAWID KRUIPER\nLOCAL MUNICIPALITY FOR THE ZF MGCAWU DISTRICT MUNICIPALITY\nDRAWINGS NOTES\nThe client accepts the fact that the contractor is a Professional builder and a master of his trade. It is\naccepted that he has visited the site and has familiarized himself with the local conditions before his tender\nwas submitted, to eliminate any possible chance that a misunderstanding might arise at a later stage due to\nhis limited knowledge of the site.\nAll work to be carried out strictly in accordance with the local authority building regulations and by-laws.\nAll materials to be SABS approved (stamped). Proof of which to be supplied (official certification) on request\nwhen applicable.\nWorkmanship to comply with the relevant National Building Regulations and Building Standards Act (Act 103\nof 1977 as amended) and the NHBRC home building Manual.\nFoundations to be inspected and approved by the engineer before concrete is poured. This also applies to\nservices in the ground.\nWall plate inspection and timber truss fixing to be approved before walls are plastered.\nTrade names specified are purely to indicate the quality and standard required. These can be substituted\nwith any product matching the quality subject to the engineer’s approval.\nThe contractor must check all the measurements and levels indicated on the drawings on site prior to the\nwork commencing.\nThe setting out of the building work remains the sole responsibility of the contractor.\nDo not scale the drawing. If uncertain about any of the dimensions, contact the Engineer.\nReport any discrepancies between the drawings and/or contract documentation to the Engineer prior to the\ncommencement of the work.\nAll the materials used must be of the best quality obtainable. The work must be done by skilled artisans and\ncarried out in such a way as to ensure the highest quality workmanship obtainable in the local industry. The\nstandard must at least be acceptable to members of the BIA and the M.B.A. Work not complying with afore\nsaid will be condemned and redone at the contractor’s expense.\nAll the materials, fittings and equipment used on the project must be applied/installed strictly according to the\nmanufacturer's specifications and guidelines.\nGreat care must be taken when installing the damp proof courses to ensure that no damaged material is\nused. All joints to have 150mm overlaps and to be taped.\nIt is important to note that the safety of any material brought onto the site and paid for by the client remains\nthe responsibility of the Contractor even though ownership has been transferred to the Client.\nAll materials on site must be stored properly to minimize any damage that might occur. Planning the work\nsequence is of the utmost importance when ordering materials to ensure that they are not stored for too long\na period.\nPlastered wall surfaces must be given sufficient time to dry out before any paintwork commences.\n\nThe contractor must insist that all site instructions be noted in writing in the site instruction book to avoid any\nlater disputes.\nContractor must familiarize himself with the 'Occupational Health and Safety act no. 85 of 1993' and fully\ncomply with the contents thereof. The safety of the public, visitors and of the workers on site is of the utmost\nimportance and remains his sole responsibility for the duration of the contract.\nThe site must be kept clean at all times and builder’s rubble removed from time to time Temporary toilet\nfacilities must be provided by the contractor for the workers and kept clean at all times.\nContractor must not deviate from the plans and must notify the Engineer of any problems immediately as\nthey arise.\nThis is to certify that I / we ………………………………………………………………………………………\nof (Tenderer) ………………………………………………………………………………………\n………………………………………………………………………………………\nof (Address) ………………………………………………………………………………………\n………………………………………………………………………………………\n………………………………………………………………………………………\nTelephone Number ………………………………………………………………………………………\nFax Number ………………………………………………………………………………………\non (Date) ………………………………………………………………………………………\nhave examined the drawing notes and its surroundings for which I/we am/are submitting this tender and\nhave, so far as is practicable, familiarized myself/ourselves with all the information, risks, contingencies and\nother circumstances which may influence or affect my/our tender.\nSIGNED ON BEHALF OF THE TENDERER: …………………………………………….…………………………\nSIGNED ON BEHALF OF THE CONSULTANT: ……………………………………………………………………\nDATE: ………………………………………………………...………\n\nPREPARATION OF BUILDING AREA\nRemove all organic material within 1.5m of perimeter of\nfoundation footprint.\nWet area to 1 - 2% of OMC (optimum moisture content). Compact\nwith (Bomag) self propelled double roller vibrator compactor a min\nof 3x over - aim for 93% mod AASHTO.
